A description of the depth- r r Bernstein center for rational depths
Sarbartha Bhattacharya, Tsao-Hsien Chen

Abstract
Let G G be a split connected reductive over a non-archimedean local field k k . In this paper we give a description of the depth- r r Bernstein center of G ⁡ ( k ) G(k) for rational depths as a limit of depth- r r standard parahoric Hecke algebras, extending our previous work in the integral depths case in [ CB24 ] . Using this description, we construct maps from the space of stable functions on depth- r r Moy-Prasad quotients to the depth- r r center, and attach depth- r r Deligne-Lusztig parameters to smooth irreducible representations, with the assignment of parameters to irreducible representations shown to be consistent with restricted Langlands parameters for Moy-Prasad types described in [ CDT25 ] . As an application, we give a decomposition of the category of smooth representations into a product of full subcategories indexed by restricted depth- r r Langlands parameters.



1
Introduction

Let
k
be a non-archimedean local field with ring of integers
\mathfrak{O}_{k}
and residue field
\kappa_{k}=\mathbb{F}_{q}
of characteristic
p
. Let
\varpi\in\mathfrak{O}_{k}
be a uniformizer and
\mathfrak{m}_{k}=\langle\varpi\mathfrak{O}_{k}\rangle
be the unique prime ideal in
\mathfrak{O}_{k}
. We fix a separable closure
\bar{k}
of
k
, and let
K=k^{u}
be the maximal unramified extension of
k
and
k^{t}
denote the maximal tamely ramified extension of
k
.
Let
G
be a connected reductive algebraic group defined over
k
. We assume throughout that
G
splits over
k
and fix a
k
-split maximal tori
T
in
G
. We denote the reduced Bruhat-Tits building of
G
by
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Further, we fix a Haar measure
\mu
on
G(k)
and identify
\mathcal{H}(G)
and compactly supported smooth functions on
G(k)
, denoted by
C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
. Let
R(G)
denote the category of smooth complex representations of
G(k)
and
\mathcal{Z}(G)=\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{Id}_{R(G)})
denote the Bernstein center. There are several equivalent descriptions of the Bernstein center, including one which interprets elements in
\mathcal{Z}(G)
as essentially compact invariant distributions on
G(k)
(for details check Section
2.3
). We denote the the set of (isomorphism classes of) smooth irreducible representations of
G(k)
by
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
. Moy and Prasad in
[
MP94
]
attached an invariant
\rho(\pi)\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
to each
\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)
, called the depth of
\pi
. For any non-negative rational number
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
,
let
R(G)_{\leq r}
(resp,
R(G)_{>r}
) denote the full subcategory of smooth representations whose irreducible subquotients have depth
\leq r
(resp, depth
>r
). Let
\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{r}
denote the set of smooth irreducible representations of
G(k)
depth
r
(similarly
\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{\leq r}
,
\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{<r}
and
\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{>r}
). Results of Bernstein and Moy-Prasad (
[
BD84
,
MP94
,
MP96
]
) imply that the category
R(G)
decomposes as a direct sum

R(G)=R(G)_{\leq r}\oplus R(G)_{>r}
, and hence the Bernstein center also decomposes as
\mathcal{Z}(G)=\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)\oplus\mathcal{Z}^{>r}(G)
. In the first part of our article, we provide a description of the depth-
r
Bernstein center
\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
.

1.1
A description of the center

In
[
BKV13
]
, Bezrukavnikov-Kazhdan-Varshavsky worked with a categorical analogue of the Bernstein center, and constructed an invariant distribution
E_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}^{0}(G)
which is the projector to the depth-zero part of the center using
l
-adic sheaves on loop groups. In our previous work
[
CB24
]
, we used some the ideas developed in
[
BKV13
]
and
[
Che23
]
in a more classical setting to give a description of the integral depth-
r
center
\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
for a split simply-connected
p
-adic group
G(k)
(see
[
CB24
, Theorem 2.8, Theorem 3.11]
). In particular, this provided a positive answer to the conjecture mentioned in the abstract of
[
Che23
]
.

In their subsequent work
[
BKV15
]
, Bezrukavnikov-Kazhdan-Varshavsky proved an explicit description for the Bernstein projector
E_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
to representations of depth
\leq r
for all rational depths. In order to deal with fractional depths, they replaced the usual simplicial structure on the building
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
with a refinement by subdividing the facets into smaller parts depending on
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
when the depth
r\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
(check Section
2.4
). Let us denote the new structure obtained by
\mathcal{B}_{m}
. The main idea in the construction of the projectors was to use a
G(k)
-equivariant system of idempotents
\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}=\mu(G_{\sigma,r+})^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{G_{\sigma,r+}}\in\mathcal{H}(G)
(see Section
2.2
for definition of
G_{\sigma,r+}
) for each refined facet
\sigma
in
\mathcal{B}_{m}
, and give a formula for the projector as an Euler-Poincaré sum of the idempotents (
[
BKV15
, Theorem 1.6]
). Some of the ideas in this work was motivated by the work of
[
MS10
]
who used a system of idempotents in
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\pi})
for
(\pi,V_{\pi})\in R(G)
to produce
G(k)
-equivariant (co)sheaves, and used that to give resolutions of certain subspaces of
V_{\pi}
.

Bernstein gave a decomposition of
R(G)
into indecomposable full subcategories
R(G)_{\mathfrak{a}}
where
\mathfrak{a}=[L,\varrho]_{G}
denotes the inertial equivalence class of
(L,\varrho)
for a
k
-Levi subgroup
L\subset G
and
\varrho
an irreducible supercuspidal representation of
L(k)
(check Section
8
for definitions and
[
Roc09
]
for more details). These subcategories are often called Bernstein components. Barbasch-Ciubotaru-Moy in
[
BCM19
]
established Euler-Poincaré formulas for projectors to individual depth-zero Bernstein components from a equivariant system of idempotents produced using cuspidal representations of the reductive quotients of parahoric subgroups, giving a decomposition of the depth-zero projector. Moy and Savin in
[
MS20
]
used similar ideas to produce a partial analogue for positive depths.

Let
[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
denote the set of refined facets obtained by subdividing each open polysimplex ( check Section
2.4
for detailed description), and
G_{\sigma,r}
be the Moy-Prasad filtration subgroups attached to
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
as described in Sections
2.2
and
2.4
. For each
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, we define

\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}^{r}:=C_{c}^{\infty}\left(\frac{G(k)/G_{\sigma,r+}}{G_{\sigma,0}}\right)

to be the algebra (under convolution with respect to
\mu
) of compactly supported smooth functions on
G(k)
which are
G_{\sigma,r+}
bi-invariant and
{G_{\sigma,0}}
conjugation invariant. Note that the idempotents
\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}
used in the construction of projectors in
[
BKV15
]
are elements in
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}^{r}
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a fixed chamber in the apartment
\mathcal{A}_{T}
corresponding to the fixed
k
-split maximal torus.

We have a partial order on
[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
given by
\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma
if
\sigma^{\prime}
is contained in the closure of
\sigma
, and this gives a partial order on
[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
. For
\sigma^{\prime},\>\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
and
\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma
, we have a map

\displaystyle\phi^{r}_{\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}:\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto f*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}

Further, for any element
n\in\mathcal{N}:=N_{G}(T)(k)
such that
n\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
, if
n\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\preceq\sigma_{2}
, we add morphisms
\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n}:\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{2}}
in the following way

\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n}:\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Ad}(n)}\mathcal{M}^{r}_{n\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{\phi^{r}_{n\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{2}}

With the above defined maps, we have an inverse system
\{\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}
and we define
A^{r}(G)
to be the inverse limit of the algebras
\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}
.

A^{r}(G):=\lim_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}

We had a similar inverse system in the description of integral depths in
[
CB24
]
, and the second set of maps were not required because we were working with simply-connected groups. Our first main result is a generalization of
[
CB24
, Theorem 1.1]
to fractional depths and split reductive groups, stated and proved in Theorem
3.8
.

Theorem 1.1
.

There is an explicit algebra isomorphism
[A^{r}]:A^{r}(G)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
for a split reductive group
G
and
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Given
h=\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
, we can define
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
and an Euler-Poincaré sum
[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}h_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(G)
for each finite convex subcomplex in
\mathcal{B}_{m}
. Let
\Theta_{m}
denote the set of non-empty finite convex subcomplexes
\Sigma\subset[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
. The main idea in the proof is to show that for every
f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
and
h\in A^{r}(G)
, the sequence
\{[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f\}_{\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}}
stabilizes (Theorem
3.1
) and use that to produce an element
[A_{h}]\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{H}(G)^{2}}(\mathcal{H}(G))\cong\mathcal{Z}(G)
. The final step is to show that the map
h\mapsto[A_{h}]
gives an algebra isomorphism
A^{r}(G)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
. The image of
\delta_{r}=\{\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
is exactly the depth-
r
projector constructed in
[
BKV15
]
.

1.2
Stable functions and Deligne-Lusztig parameters

In
[
CDT25
]
, Chen-Debacker-Tsai attached Deligne-Lusztig parameters (see sections
6.1
and
7.3
for the definition) to Moy-Prasad types and proved that these parameters are the same for any two Moy-Prasad types contained in a smooth irreducible representation. Similar parameters were studied in
[
CB24
]
for split simply-connected groups, where they were called semi-simple part of a minimal
K
-type. Further, they established a connection with the Galois side and showed that these parameters are in bijection to restricted Langlands parameters which are continuous homomorphisms
I_{k}^{r}/I^{r+}_{k}\rightarrow G^{\vee}
satisfying certain properties (see Definitions
7.13
and
6.7
), where
I_{k}^{r}
denotes the upper numbering filtration of the Weil group
W_{k}
of the local field
k
, and
G^{\vee}
denotes the complex dual group of
G
. For
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, they define a map
\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{r}\rightarrow\mathrm{DL}_{r}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{RP}_{r}
where
\mathrm{DL}_{r}
and
\mathrm{RP}_{r}
denote the Deligne-Lusztig and restricted Langlands parameters of depth-
r
respectively. The Deligne-Lusztig parameters decmposes the set
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
into disjoint sets and conjecturally, these sets are unions of
L
-packets. The main aim of the subsequent sections is to use the description of the center in Section
3
to construct elements in
\mathcal{Z}(G)
which act by the same constant on smooth irreducible representations having the same Deligne-Lusztig(DL) parameter attached to it.

To that end, in section
4
, we define and study stable functions on positive depth Moy-Prasad quotients. For positive integral depths, they were studied in
[
CB24
]
, inspired by similar concepts in
[
LL23
]
and
[
Che22
]
.
In the case of positive integral depths, the Moy-Prasad filtration quotients at a point in the Bruhat-Tits building are isomorphic to the Lie algebra of the reductive quotient of the parahoric subgroup. The main tool in studying and contructing stable functions in this case was Fourier transforms on finite Lie algebras studied in
[
Let96
]
. To deal with fractional depths, we develop a theory of Fourier transforms on fractional depth quotients in Section
4.2
, extending the results in
[
Let96
]
and use that to construct stable functions on fractional depth quotients for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}\cap\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.

For the fixed maximal tori
T
, let
\mathbb{T}
be the reductive quotient of
T(k^{t})
, which is an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-split
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-torus canonically identified with the reductive quotient of
T(K)
since
T
is
k
-split, and let
\bar{\mathfrak{t}}=\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{T})
. The space
(\bar{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}//W)^{F}
where
F
denotes the (geometric) Frobenius and
W
the Weyl group of
T
is the parameter space for depth-
r
Deligne Lusztig parameters (along with some other data, see Section
6.1
). In Section
5
, we use the theory of stable functions to construct a elements in the depth-
r
center via a map described in Theorem
5.7
whose main statement is the following:

Theorem 1.2
.

There is an algebra homomorphism

\xi^{r}:\mathbb{C}[(\bar{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}//W)^{F}]\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)

This enables us to attach Deligne-Lusztig parameters to smooth irreducible representations of positive depth and define a map
\Theta_{r}:\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{r}\rightarrow\mathrm{DL}_{r}
for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}\cap\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
, which only takes values in non-trivial parameters, which are denoted by
\mathrm{DL}^{\circ}_{r}
. We further show in Proposition
6.5
that the parameters we attach are the same as the ones in
[
CDT25
]
, and hence our construction produces elements in the center which act by the same constant on smooth irreducible representations having the same DL parameter. We deal with the depth zero case and show similar results in Section
7
, constructing a map
\Theta_{0}:\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{DL}_{0}
. Most of the ideas regarding stable functions in this depth zero was developed in
[
CB24
]
, and we have generalized them to the reductive case.

With the additional assumption that
p\nmid|W|
, we know that smooth irreducible representations have depth in
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}\cap\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
. Thus we have a map
\Theta:\mathrm{Irr}(G)\rightarrow\mathrm{DL}^{t}:=\mathrm{DL}_{0}\coprod\left(\coprod_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}\cap\mathbb{Q}_{>0}}\mathrm{DL}_{r}^{\circ}\right)
and we can partition
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
into packets
\Pi(\vartheta),\>\vartheta\in\mathrm{DL}^{t}
having the same Deligne-Lusztig parameter. The maps in Theorem
5.7
and
7.7
enable us to construct projectors to the packets (Proposition
8.6
) and decompose the category
R(G)
into a product of full subcategories (Theorem
8.3
)

R(G)=\prod_{\vartheta\in\mathrm{DL}^{t}}R(G)_{\vartheta}

(1.2.1)

where
R(G)_{\vartheta}
is the full subcategory of
R(G)
consisting of those representations all of whose irreducible subquotients are in
\Pi(\vartheta)
.
The decomposition in (
1.2.1
) then gives a decomposition
R(G)_{\leq r}
in terms of restricted Langlands parameters
:

Theorem 1.3
.

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}\cap\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, we have a decomposition

R(G)_{\leq r}=\bigoplus_{\varphi^{r}\in\mathrm{RP}_{r}}R(G)_{\varphi_{r}}

of
R(G)_{\leq r}
into a product of full subcategories indexed by restricted
depth-
r
Langlands parameters
\mathrm{RP}_{r}
.

The theorem is restated in
Corollary
8.8
.
In the case of positive depths, the subcategory corresponding to the to the trivial depth-
r
parameter contains the smooth representations all of whose irreducible subquotients are in
\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{<r}
.

1.3
A conjecture on stability

An element in the Bernstein center is called stable if the associated invariant distribution is stable. Let
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)
denote the vector subspace of stable elements in the center, and
\mathcal{Z}^{st,r}(G)=\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)\cap\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
. The stable center conjecture asserts that
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)\subset\mathcal{Z}(G)
is a unital sub-algebra. The stable center and some of its conjectural properties and equivalent interpretations are described briefly Section
2.3
. We have the following conjecture about the elements in
\mathcal{Z}(G)
that lie in the image of
\xi^{r}
under the assumption that the residue chracteristic is large enough, which we state in detail with some evidence in Section
9
.

Conjecture 1.1
.

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}\cap\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
, we have
\mathrm{Im}(\xi^{r})\subset\mathcal{Z}^{st,r}(G)
.

In
[
BKV13
]
, Bezrukavnikov-Kazhdan-Varshavsky use the geometry of affine Springer fibers and
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves to
study the depth-zero stable center conjecture. The results of the paper
provide a possible approach to the
positive depth stable center conjecture using the geometric framework
developed in
loc. cit.
.

1.4
Organization and some conventions

We briefly summarize the main goals of each section. In Section
2
, we give some necessary background about Bruhat-Tits buildings, Moy-Prasad filtrations, the Bernstein center and set up some notations used throughout the article. Section
3
gives a description of the depth-
r
center for non negative rational depths, generalizing previous results in the integral depth case. In Section
4
, we define and study stable functions on positive depth Moy-Prasad quotients, using the theory of Fourier transforms on such quotients. In Section
5
, we use the stable functions to construct a map from the space of functions on depth-
r
Deligne-Lusztig parameters to the depth-
r
center. The maps to the depth-
r
center constructed in the previous section are used to attach Deligne-Lusztig parameters to smooth irreducible representations of positive depth in Section
6
. We also give a brief description of their relation to restricted Langlands parameters. Section
7
studies the same results for the depth-zero case. We decompose the category of smooth representations into full subcategories in Section
8
using the partition of
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
into disjoint sets via their Deligne-Lusztig parameters. Finally, in the last section, we have some conjectures about the stability of the elements in the center that we constructed.

Some conventions:

Throughout the paper, we assume that
p>2
. In some sections, there are additional assumptions on
p
. We use
\mathfrak{F}
to denote a (geometric) Frobenius in the local field setting, for example in
W_{k}
or in
\Gal(K/k)
, and
F
to denote the (geometric) Frobenius in
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-vector spaces or varieties with
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure. We generally always use the geometric Frobenius unless mentioned otherwise

Acknowledgement

The authors thank Connor Bass, Charlotte Chan, Stephen DeBacker and Cheng-Chiang Tsai
for many useful discussions. T.-H. Chen also thanks the
NCTS-National Center for Theoretical Sciences at Taipei
where parts of this work were done. The research of T.-H. Chen is supported by NSF grant DMS-2143722.

2
Background and some notations

2.1
Buildings- The split case

We fix a discrete valuation on
\mathrm{v}:k\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
, which extends uniquely to
\mathrm{v}:\bar{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\mathrm{v}(K)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
. Let
S
be a
k
-split maximal torus in
G
, and
X^{*}(S)=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(S,\mathbb{G}_{m})
and
X_{*}(S)=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(\mathbb{G}_{m},S)
denote the lattice of
k
-rational characters and co-characters of
S
respectively. Further, let
\Phi(G,S)
and
\Phi^{\vee}(G,S)
denote the root and co-root lattice of
G
with respect to
S
, which can be identified with the absolute root system since
S
is
k
-split. Henceforth, we will denote them by
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\vee}
. We have a perfect pairing

<,>:X_{*}(S)\times X^{*}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

(2.1.1)

where
<\lambda,\chi>
denotes the integer such that
\chi\circ\lambda(s)=s^{<\lambda,\chi>}
. Let
V_{1}=X_{*}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
, and we identify
V_{1}^{*}
and
X^{*}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
. The pairing in (
2.1.1
) canonically extends to
<,>:V_{1}^{*}\times V_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. There is a unique group homomorphism
\nu_{1}:S(k)\rightarrow V_{1}
such that

<\nu_{1}(s),\chi>=-\mathrm{v}(\chi(s))

(2.1.2)

for all
s\in S(k)
and
\chi\in X^{*}(S)
. Let
S_{b}(k)
denote the kernel of
\nu_{1}
. Note that in this case
S_{b}(k)=S(\mathfrak{O}_{k})
. Let
N=N_{G}(S)
.
S(k)
is a normal subgroup of
N(k)
, and we can immediately observe that
S_{b}(k)
is also a normal subgroup of
N(k)
since
\prescript{n}{}{}\chi\in X^{*}(S)
for
n\in N(k)
. Thus, we have the exact sequence

0\rightarrow S(k)/S_{b}(k)\rightarrow N(k)/S_{b}(k)\rightarrow N(k)/S(k)\rightarrow 1

The group
N(k)/S(k)
is the Weyl group of the root system
\Phi
and acts naturally on the vector spaces
V_{1}
and
V_{1}^{*}
. The first group in the sequence
S(k)/S_{b}(k)
is a free abelian group of rank dim
V_{1}
(check
[
Lan06
, Lemma 1.3 ]
). Let
W_{S}=N(k)/S(k)
,
\Lambda=S(k)/S_{b}(k)
and
\widetilde{W}_{S}=N(k)/S_{b}(k)
.
Consider the subspace
V_{0}\subset V_{1}
defined by

V_{0}:=\{v\in V_{1}\>|\>\alpha(v)=0\>\forall\>\alpha\in\Phi\}\cong X_{*}(Z(G))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}

The group
N(k)/S(k)
acts trivially on
V_{0}
and
V_{0}=0
if
G
is semisimple. Let
V=V(G,S,k)
denote the vector space
V_{1}/V_{0}
and
\nu:S(k)\rightarrow V
be the composition
S(k)\xrightarrow{\nu_{1}}V_{1}\twoheadrightarrow V
. Note that
V\cong\Phi^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
and the canonical group homomorphism
j_{1}:W_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(V_{1})
induces a group homomorphism
j:W_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(V)
since the image of
j_{1}
acts trivially on
V_{0}
.

Let
A
be an affine space over
V
and
\mathrm{Aff}(A)
denote the group of affine isomorphism
A\rightarrow A
. There is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow V\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(A)\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{GL}(V)\rightarrow 1

which splits non-canonically (depending upon choice of
x\in A
) and hence
\mathrm{Aff}(A)\cong V\rtimes\mathrm{GL}(V)
(check
[
KP23
, Section 1.2]
). We have an extension of groups

0\rightarrow\Lambda\rightarrow\widetilde{W}_{S}\rightarrow W_{S}\rightarrow 1

which represents a class in
H^{2}(W_{S},\Lambda)
. Using the map
\Lambda\xhookrightarrow{\nu}V
and functoriality of
H^{2}(-,W_{S})
, we have its image in
H^{2}(V,W_{S})
given by

0\rightarrow V\rightarrow\widetilde{W}_{S}^{\prime}\rightarrow W_{S}\rightarrow 1

However,
H^{2}(V,W_{S})=0
, and hence the second extension is trivial, i.e.,
\widetilde{W}_{S}^{\prime}\cong V\rtimes W_{S}
.
Since
\nu(w_{1}\cdot l\cdot w_{1}^{-1})=j(\bar{w}_{1})(\nu(l))
, for
w_{1}\in\widetilde{W}_{S}
,
l\in\Lambda
where
\bar{w}_{1}
denotes the image of
w_{1}
in
W_{S}
, we note that the map
j:W_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(V)
is induced by
W_{S}\rightarrow GL_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Lambda)
and hence the map
W_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(V)
in the semi-direct product is given by
j
. Thus, there an affine space
A
over
V
and a map
\tilde{f}:V\rtimes W_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(A)
such that
\tilde{f}((v,1))
is translation by
v\in V
and
d(f(v,w))=j(w)
. This gives a map
f:\widetilde{W}_{S}\rightarrow V\rtimes W_{S}\xrightarrow{f}\mathrm{Aff}(A)
such that
f(\lambda)
is translation by
\nu(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
d(f(w_{1}))=j(\bar{w}_{1})
for
w_{1}\in\widetilde{W}_{S}
, which fits into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{W}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aff}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GL}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

So, we have an affine space
A
over
V
and a map
N(k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(A)
extending
\nu:S(k)\rightarrow V
, and by
[
Lan06
, Proposition 1.8]

such a pair is unique upto unique isomorphism. Hence we denote the unique map
N(k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(A)
by
\nu
as well. As mentioned in
[
Yu09b
, Section 2.2]
the obstruction to existence of an isomorphism lies in
H^{1}(W_{S},V)
which is
0
, and the obstruction to its uniqueness lies in
H^{0}(W_{S},V)=V^{W_{S}}=0
. The affine space
\mathcal{A}(G,S):=\mathcal{A}_{S}:=A
along with the group homomorphism
\nu:N(k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(\mathcal{A}_{S})
is called the (reduced) apartment of
G
with respect to
S
.

Note that if we were working with
V_{1}
instead of
V
, the pair would have been unique, but not upto unique isomorphism. Working with the full vector space
V_{1}
gives an affine space often called the extended apartment. The exteneded apartment is canonical in the case of semisimple groups, in which case
V=V_{1}
.

We will give a concrete realization of the apartment, and this is the notion we will use most often. The apartment
\mathcal{A}_{S}
is an affine space under
V\cong\Phi^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
, and the pair
(\mathcal{A}_{S},\nu)
is unique upto unique isomorphism. Hence, it is enough to give one description of it.

For
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
U_{\alpha}
denote the root subgroup of
G
with respect to
S
, and
r_{\alpha}
denote the reflection with respect to
\alpha
in the Weyl group of
\Phi
. A system
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
of
k
-group isomorphisms
\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow U_{\alpha}
is called a
k
-épinglage of
G
with respect to
S
. Two
k
isomorphisms
x_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow U_{\alpha}

and
x_{-\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow U_{-\alpha}
are said to be associated if there is a
k
-group monomorphism
\varepsilon_{\alpha}:SL_{2}\rightarrow G
such that for
y\in\mathbb{G}_{a}(k)=k
, the following conditions hold :

x_{\alpha}(y)=\varepsilon_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}1&y\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }x_{-\alpha}(y)=\varepsilon_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-y&1\end{pmatrix}

Note that
\varepsilon_{\alpha}
is uniquely determined by this condition and
m_{\alpha}:=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{-\alpha}(1)x_{\alpha}(1)=\varepsilon_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
is in the normalizer
N_{G}(S)(k)
of the maximal torus
S
.

A system
\{x_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\xrightarrow{\simeq}U_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Phi}
of
k
-group isomorphisms is called a
k
-Chevalley system of
G
(with respect to
S
) if the following proprties are true.

•

x_{\alpha}
and
x_{-\alpha}
are associated for all
\alpha\in\Phi
.

•

For
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
, there exists
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that for all
y\in\mathbb{G}_{a}(k)
, we have

x_{r_{\alpha}(\beta)}(y)=m_{\alpha}\cdot x_{\beta}(\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot y)m_{\alpha}^{-1}

As per our convention
\epsilon_{\alpha,\alpha}=1
.

We fix a Chevalley system
\{x_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\xrightarrow{\simeq}U_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Phi}
of
G
with respect to
S
. We can define a valuation
\varphi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}(k)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
of
U_{\alpha}(k)
by

\varphi_{\alpha}(u)=\mathrm{v}(x_{\alpha}^{-1}(u))\text{ for }u\in U_{\alpha}(k)

This valuation corresponds to a special point in the apartment
\mathcal{A}(S,k)
denoted by
x_{0}
, which is the point fixed by
m_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
. Fixing the point
x_{0}
identifies the affine space
\mathcal{A}(S,k)
with
V
, with the point
x_{0}+v
corresponding to the valuation
\tilde{\{}\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\}_{\alpha\in\Phi}
given by
\tilde{\varphi}_{\alpha}(u)=\varphi_{\alpha}(u)+\alpha(v)
. The apartment
\mathcal{A}(S,k)
can be defined as the affine space defined by the set of all such valuations
\tilde{\{}\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\}_{\alpha\in\Phi}
. The map
\nu|_{S(k)}
is already defined as in (
2.1.2
), and it is enough to specify the actions of
W_{S}
on
V
to give
\nu:N(k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(\mathcal{A}_{S})
. Note that
m_{\alpha}
maps to the reflections
r_{\alpha}
under the isomorphism of
N(k)/S(k)
with the Weyl group of
\Phi
, and
r_{\alpha}\in W_{S}
has the obvious natural action on
V
, which finishes the definition of the pair
(\mathcal{A}_{S},\nu)
.

Remark 2.1
.

As mentioned in
[
Fin19
, Remark 2.1.1]
, similar definitions can be made without the associated condition for each root
\alpha
, and we can let
\epsilon_{\alpha,\alpha}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
m_{\alpha}=x_{\alpha}(1)x_{-\alpha}(\epsilon_{\alpha,\alpha})x_{\alpha}(1)
is contained in the normalizer of
S
.

For
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
\Gamma_{\alpha}:=\{\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\>|\>u\in U_{\alpha}(k)\setminus\{1\}\}=\mathbb{Z}
. The set of affine roots
\Psi(\mathcal{A}_{S})=\Psi(G,S)
on
\mathcal{A}_{S}
consists of affine functions given by

\Psi(\mathcal{A}_{S})=\{x\mapsto\alpha(x-x_{0})+\gamma\>|\>\alpha\in\Phi,\;\gamma\in\Gamma_{\alpha}\}

We often denote the affine function
x\mapsto\alpha(x-x_{0})+n
by
\alpha+n
. For affine function on
\mathcal{A}_{S}
of the form
\psi=\alpha+l
,
l\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\dot{\psi}=\nabla\psi:=\alpha
denote and
H_{\psi}:=\{x\in\mathcal{A}_{S}\;|\>\psi(x)=0\}
. The hyperplanes
\{H_{\psi}\>|\>\psi\in\Psi(\mathcal{A}_{S})\}
are affine subspaces of codimension
1
, often called a wall and they give
\mathcal{A}_{S}
the structure of a poly-simplicial complex. The connected componenets of
\mathcal{A}_{S}\setminus\cup_{\psi\in\Psi(\mathcal{A}_{S})}H_{\psi}
are called chambers. Two points
x,y\in\mathcal{A}_{S}
are called equivalent if for all affine roots
\psi
,
\psi(x)
and
\psi(y)
have the same sign or are both
0
. The equivalence classes are called facets, and two points are in the same facet if they belong to the same (open) poly-simplex. The apartment
\mathcal{A}_{S}
can be written as a disjoint union of these open polysimplices. There is a
W_{S}
-invariant scalar product on
V
, and if we equip
\mathcal{A}_{S}
with the metric defined by the scalar product, the
N(k)
action on
\mathcal{A}_{S}
becomes isometrical.

Using affine functions of the form
\alpha+l
for
l\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain a filtration of the root subgroups
U_{\alpha}(k)
for
\alpha\in\Phi
. For
\psi=\alpha+l
we define

U_{\psi}(k)=\{u\in U_{\dot{\psi}}(k)\>|\>u=1\text{ or }\varphi_{\dot{\psi}}(u)\geq\psi(x_{0})\}=\{x_{\alpha}(y)\>|\>\mathrm{v}(y)\geq l\}\cup\{1\}

often denoted by
U_{\alpha,l}
as well. We can similarly define
U_{\psi+}
as

U_{\psi+}(k)=\{u\in U_{\dot{\psi}}(k)\>|\>u=1\text{ or }\varphi_{\dot{\psi}}(u)>\psi(x_{0})\}=\{x_{\alpha}(y)\>|\>\mathrm{v}(y)>l\}\cup\{1\}

For a bounded subset
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}_{S}
let
f_{\Omega}:\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined as
f_{\Omega}(\alpha)=\mathrm{inf}\{l\in\mathbb{R}\>|\>\alpha(x-x_{0}+l\geq 0\>\forall\>x\in\Omega\}
. We define
U_{\alpha,\Omega}(k):=U_{\alpha,f_{\Omega}(\alpha)}(k)
for
\alpha\in\Phi
and
U_{\Omega}(k):=\langle U_{\alpha,\Omega}(k)\>|\>\alpha\in\Phi\rangle\subset G(k)
. For
\Omega=\{s\}
, we often denote
U_{\Omega}
by
U_{x}
.

Consider the equivalence relation on
G(k)\times\mathcal{A}_{S}
defined by
(g,x)\sim(h,y)
if there is an element
n\in N(k)
with
y=\nu(n)(x)
and
g^{-1}hn\in U_{x}
. Let
\mathcal{B}(G,k):=(G(k)\times\mathcal{A}_{S})/\sim
. The canonical map

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{S}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(G,k)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto[(1,x)]

where
[(1,x)]
denotes the equivalence class of
(1,x)
is injective and hence we can identify
\mathcal{A}_{S}
with its image in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. We have a
G(k)
-action on
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
given by

\displaystyle G(k)\times\mathcal{B}(G,k)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(G,k)

\displaystyle(g,[(h,x)])

\displaystyle\mapsto[(gh,x)]

The subsets of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
of the form
g\mathcal{A}_{S}
for
g\in G(k)
are called apartments. The apartment
g\mathcal{A}_{S}
coincides with the apartment
\mathcal{A}(gSg^{-1},k)
. The stabilizer of
\mathcal{A}_{S}
in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is
N(k)
, and the map
S\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{S}
is a
G(k)
equivariant bijection between
k
-split maximal tori of
G
and the set of apartments of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. A subset
Y\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is called a facet (resp. chamber) if there exists
g\in G(k)
such that
gY\subset\mathcal{A}_{S}
is a facet (resp. chamber). This gives the building a polysimplicial structure, and it is a union of these (open) polysimplices. We fix a
W_{S}
-invariant scalar product on
V
and consider the induced metric on
\mathcal{A}_{S}
. There is a unique metric on
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
which is
G(k)
-invariant and coincides with the one on
\mathcal{A}_{S}
(check
[
Lan06
, Section 13.14]
). The group
G(k)
acts via isometries on
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. The
G(k)
-set
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
along with the polysimplicial structure is called the Bruhat-Tits building of
G
.

2.2
Moy-Prasad Filtrations

Filtrations for split Tori and its Lie algebra.

Let
S
be a split torus,
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{Lie}(S)
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. We define

S(k)_{0}=\{s\in S(k)\>|\>\mathrm{v}(\chi(t))=0\>\forall\>\chi\in X^{*}_{\bar{k}}(S)\}

In the split case since
\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(S,\mathbb{G}_{m})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\bar{k}}(S,\mathbb{G}_{m})
, we have that
S_{b}(k)=S(k)_{0}
. In the general cases,
S(k)_{0}
is a finite index subgroup of
S_{b}(k)
. Henceforth, we will identify them. The torus
S
has a natural structure over
\mathfrak{O}_{k}
, and
S(k)_{0}=S(\mathfrak{O}_{k})
. For any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we define

S(k)_{r}=\{s\in S(k)_{0}\>|\>\mathrm{v}(\chi(t)-1)\geq r\>\forall\>\chi\in X^{*}(S)\}

and
S(k)_{r+}=\cup_{r_{1}>r}S(k)_{r_{1}}=\{s\in S(k)_{0}\>|\>\mathrm{v}(\chi(t)-1)>r\>\forall\>\chi\in X^{*}(S)\}
. For the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{s}
of
S
, we can similarly define a filtration by
\mathfrak{O}_{k}
-modules

\mathfrak{s}(k)_{r}=\{X\in\mathfrak{s}(k)\>|\>\mathrm{v}(d\chi(X))\geq r\>\forall\chi\in X^{*}(S)\}

Filtrations of
G(k)
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{A}_{S}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we can define a filtration of the root group
U_{\alpha}(k)
depending on
x
as follows:

U_{\alpha}(k)_{x,r}:=x_{\alpha}(\varpi^{\lceil r-\alpha(x-x_{0})\rceil}\mathfrak{O}_{k})

We define the Moy-Prasad filtration subgroups
G(k)_{x,r}
of
G(k)
as

G(k)_{x,r}=\langle S(k)_{r},U_{\psi}(k)\>|\psi\in\Psi(\mathcal{A}_{S}),\;\psi(x)\geq r\rangle=\langle S(k)_{r},\;U_{\alpha}(k)_{x,r}\>|\>\alpha\in\Phi(G,S)\rangle

The subgroups
G(k)_{x,0}\subset G(k)
for
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
are called parahoric subgroups of
G(k)
corresponding to
x
. We set
G(k)_{x,r+}=\cup_{r_{1}>r}G(k)_{x,r_{1}}
. When the ground field is clear, we will denote
G(k)_{x,r}
and
G(k)_{x,r+}
by
G_{x,r}
and
G_{x,r+}
respectively.

Filtrations of the Lie algebra and its dual

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
,
\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}=\mathrm{Lie}(U_{\alpha})
and
X_{\alpha}=dx_{\alpha}(1)
where
dx_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}
is the derivative of
x_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow U_{\alpha}
. For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we can define
filtrations
\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}(k)_{x,r}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}(k)
depending on
x\in\mathcal{A}_{S}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
by

\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}(k)_{x,r}=\varpi^{\lceil r-\alpha(x-x_{0})\rceil}\mathfrak{O}_{k}X_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}(k)

Note that given an affine function
\psi=\alpha+l
,
l\in\mathbb{R}
, we can define a filtration
\mathfrak{u}_{\psi}(k)
of
\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}(k)
similarly. Then, we can define the Moy-Prasad filtrations of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}(k)
by

\mathfrak{g}(k)_{x,r}=\mathfrak{s}(k)_{r}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi}\mathfrak{u}_{\alpha}(k)_{x,r}\right)=\langle\mathfrak{s}(k)_{r},\mathfrak{u}_{\psi}\>|\>\psi\in\Psi(\mathcal{A}_{S}),\;\psi(x)\geq r\rangle

and
\mathfrak{g}(k)_{x,r+}=\cup_{r_{1}>r}\mathfrak{g}(k)_{x,r+}
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}^{*}=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathfrak{g},k)
denote the dual of
\mathfrak{g}
. We also have the Moy-Prasad filtration subspaces of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}(k)
, defined in the following way :

\mathfrak{g}^{*}(k)_{x,-r}=\{X^{*}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}(k)\>|\>X^{*}(Y)\in\varpi\mathfrak{O}_{k}\>\forall\>Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{x,r+}\}

(2.2.1)

and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}(k)_{x,-r+}=\cup_{r_{1}<r}\mathfrak{g}^{*}(k)_{x,-r_{1}}
. When the ground field is clear, we will denote
\mathfrak{g}^{*}(k)_{x,-r}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}(k)_{x,-r+}
by
\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{x,-r}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{x,-r+}
respectively.

2.3
The Bernstein center

We fix a Haar measure
\mu
of
G(k)
and let
\mathcal{H}(G):=(C_{c}^{\infty}(G),*)
be the Hecke algebra of compactly supported smooth functions on
G(k)
with multiplication given by the convolution product

f*g(x)=\int_{G(k)}f(xy^{-1})g(y)d\mu(y)

(2.3.1)

The Bernstein center
\mathcal{Z}(G)
of
G(k)
is defined as the algebra of endomorphisms of the identity functor
\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{Id}_{R(G)})
in the category
R(G)
of smooth representations of a
p
-adic group. There are several equivalent ways of describing
\mathcal{Z}(G)
, and we give a brief review of them following
[
BKV13
]
,
[
Hai14
]
.

A distribution is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map
D:C_{c}^{\infty}(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. For
f\in C^{\infty}(G)
, let
\breve{f}
denote the function
\breve{f}(x)=f(x^{-1})
. We define
\breve{D}(f)=D(\breve{f})
for a distribution
D
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
. The convolution of a distribution with a function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
can be defined by

(D*f)(g)=\breve{D}(g\cdot f)

where
g\cdot f(x)=f(xg)
. Note that
D*f\in C^{\infty}(G)
. A distribution
D
is said to be essentially compact if
D*f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
. Let
\prescript{g}{}{}f(x)=f(g^{-1}xg)
. A distribution is said to be
G(k)
-invariant if
D(\prescript{g}{}{}f)=D(f)\>\forall\>f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G),\>g\in G(k)
. The set of essentially compact
G(k)
-invariant distributions is denoted by
\mathcal{D}(G)^{G}_{ec}
and it is a an associative and commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra (check
[
Hai14
, Corollary 3.1.2]
) with convolution product defined in the following way:

(D_{1}*D_{2})(f)=\breve{D}_{1}(D_{2}*f)

Note that this only works for essentially compact distributions.

Given
(\pi,V)\in R(G)
, each
z\in\mathcal{Z}(G)
defines an endomorphism
z|_{V}\in\text{End}_{G(k)}(V)
. In particular, if
(\pi,V)\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)
, each
z\in\mathcal{Z}(G)
defines a function
f_{z}:Irr(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by Schur’s Lemma such that
z|_{V}=f_{z}(\pi)\mathrm{Id}_{V}
. Moreover, the map
z\mapsto f_{z}
is an algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{Z}(G)\rightarrow Fun(\>\mathrm{Irr}(G),\>\mathbb{C})
, which is injective.

Any smooth
G(k)
-representation is equivalently a non-degenerate
\mathcal{H}(G)
-module. Let
(l,\mathcal{H}(G))
and
(r,\mathcal{H}(G))
denote the smooth
G(k)
-representations induced by left and right translations by
G(k)
on
\mathcal{H}(G)
. The action on
G(k)
by
G(k)^{2}
, defined by
(g,h)(x)=gxh^{-1}
gives a
G(k)^{2}
action on
\mathcal{H}(G)
, given by
(g,h)f(x)=l(g)r(h)f(x)=f(g^{-1}xh)
, and hence
\mathcal{H}(G)^{2}
-module structure on
\mathcal{H}(G)
. Note that the actions
l
and
r
commute, and the action of
\mathcal{H}(G)^{2}
on
\mathcal{H}(G)
is given by
(\alpha,\beta)f=\alpha*f*\breve{\beta}
, where
\breve{\beta}(x)=\beta(x^{-1})
. Each
z\in\mathcal{Z}(G)
defines an endomorphism
z_{\mathcal{H}}
of the smooth representation
(l,\mathcal{H}(G))
, and since the actions
l
and
r
commute, the endomorphism
z_{\mathcal{H}}
of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(G)
commutes with left and right
G(k)
-actions and hence left and right convolutions. For every
(\pi,V)\in R(G)
,
v\in V
and
h\in\mathcal{H}(G)
, we have the equality
z_{V}(h(v))=(z_{\mathcal{H}}(h))(v)
. Moreover, the map
z\mapsto z_{\mathcal{H}}
defines an algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}(G)\xrightarrow{\sim}\text{End}_{\mathcal{H}(G)^{2}}(\mathcal{H}(G))
.

An element
z\in\mathcal{Z}(G)
defines an endomorphism
z_{reg}
of the
G(k)
representation on
\mathcal{H}(G)
given by the conjugation action, and hence gives rise to an
G(k)
-invariant distribution
\nu_{z}
such that
\nu_{z}(f)=z_{reg}(\breve{f})(1)
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
. The invariant distribution
\nu_{z}
can be characterised by the condition
\nu_{z}*h=z_{\mathcal{H}}(h)\>\forall\>h\in\mathcal{H}(G)
. Moreover, the map
z\mapsto\nu_{z}
gives an isomorphism of
\mathcal{Z}(G)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{D}(G)^{G}_{ec}
.

For a compact open subgroup
K\subset G(k)
, let
\delta_{K}\in\mathcal{H}(G)
be defined as
\delta_{K}:=\mu(K)^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{K}
, where
\mathbbm{1}_{K}
is the characteristic function of
K
. We denote by
\mathcal{H}(G,K)=\delta_{K}*\mathcal{H}(G)*\delta_{K}
the convolution algebra of
K
bi-invariant compactly supported smooth functions on
G(k)
. Let
\mathcal{Z}(G,K)=Z(\mathcal{H}(G,K))
denote the center of
\mathcal{H}(G,K)
, and they form a projective system with maps given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(G,K)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}(G,K^{\prime})

\displaystyle z_{K}

\displaystyle\mapsto z_{K}*\delta_{K^{\prime}}

for
K^{\prime}\subset K
. An element of
\varprojlim_{K}\mathcal{Z}(G,K)
acts on objects of
R(G)
in a way that commutes with the
\mathcal{H}(G)
-action, and we have an isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}(G)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\varprojlim_{K}\mathcal{Z}(G,K)
, where
K
runs over all compact open subgroups of
G(k)
. The center
\mathcal{Z}(G)
can also be described as the ring of regular functions
\mathbb{C}[\Omega(G)]
on the variety of (super)cuspidal supports
\Omega(G)
(see Section
8
and
[
Hai14
, Section 3.3]
).

Stable Bernstein center.

An element
x\in G
is said to be strongly regular semisimple if the stabilizer
G_{x}:=\mathrm{Stab}_{G}(x)\subset G
is a maximal torus. Let
G^{sr}
denote the set of strongly regular semisimple elements of
G
. It is an open subvariety of
G
. For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
and
x\in G^{sr}(k)
, we can define the normalized orbital integral of
f
at
x
by

O_{x}(f)=|D_{G}(x)|^{1/2}\int_{G(k)/G_{x}(k)}f(gxg^{-1})d\dot{g}

where
D_{G}(x)
is the Weyl discriminant of
x
in
G(k)
and
d\dot{g}
is the left
G(k)
-invariant Haar measure on
G(k)/G_{x}(k)
induced by Haar measures on
G(k)
and
G_{x}(k)
. When
x
is semisimple,
G_{x}(k)
is unimodular and hence
G(k)/G_{x}(k)
does carry a
G(k)
-invariant measure. Note that
G^{sr}(k)
is invariant under stable conjugacy, and we can define the stable orbital integral of
f
at
x
by

O^{st}_{x}(f):=\sum_{x\sim_{st}x^{\prime}/\sim}O_{x^{\prime}}(f)

where the sum is over representatives of
G(k)
-conjugacy classes is the stable conjugacy class of
x
. This defines a
G(k)
-invariant distribution
O^{st}_{x}
, determined uniquely upto a constant. A function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
is called unstable if
O_{x}^{st}(f)=0
for all
x\in G^{sr}(k)
, and an invariant distribution
D
is called stable if
D(f)=0
for every unstable
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
. An element
z\in\mathcal{Z}(G)
in the Bernstein center is called stable if the associated distribution
\nu_{z}\in\mathcal{D}(G)^{G}_{ec}
is a stable distribution. We denote by
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)
the vector subspace of stable elements in the Bernstein center
\mathcal{Z}(G)
.

The stable center conjecture states that
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)\subset\mathcal{Z}(G)
is a subalgebra, and there is a decomposition of the set
\mathrm{Irr}(G)
of irreducible representations of
G(k)
into packets via characters of
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)
, which is slightly coarser than the conjectural decomposition into
L
-packets as per the Local Langlands correspondence (check
[
BKV13
, Section 3.1.4]
).

An element
z\in\mathcal{Z}(G)
is called very stable if
\nu_{z}*f
is unstable for every unstable
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
. We denote the set of very stable elements in
\mathcal{Z}(G)
by
\mathcal{Z}^{vst}(G)
, and it is a commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra contained in
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)
. A stronger form of the stable center conjecture suggests that
\mathcal{Z}^{vst}(G)
and
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)
coincides (check
[
Han25
, Section 1.1]
).

Let
W_{k}
denote the Weil group of the local field
k
, and
WD_{k}=W_{k}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
denote the Weil-Deligne group. Further let
G^{\vee}
denote the complex dual group of
G
and
\prescript{L}{}{}G=G^{\vee}\ltimes W_{k}
denote the
L
-group. The
G^{\vee}
conjugacy class of an admissible (in the sense of
[
Hai14
, Section 5]
) homomorphism
\lambda:W_{k}\rightarrow\prescript{L}{}{}G
is called an infinitesimal character. Let
\Omega(\prescript{L}{}{}G)
denote the variety of infinitesimal characters as defined in
[
Hai14
, Section 5.3]
. Under the assumption that Local Langlands correspondence for
G
and its Levi subgroups is known, and some compatibility of LLC with normalized parabolic induction (check LLC+ as defined in
[
Hai14
, Definition 5.2.1]
), Haines showed in
[
Hai14
, Proposition 5.5.1]
that there is a morphism of algebraic varieties
p_{1}:\Omega(G)\rightarrow\Omega(\prescript{L}{}{}G)
which is surjective when
G
is quasi-split. Hence, we get an embedding
\mathbb{C}[\Omega(\prescript{L}{}{}G)]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\Omega(G)]\cong\mathcal{Z}(G)
, and
\mathbb{C}[\Omega(\prescript{L}{}{}G)]
is defined in
[
Hai14
, Section 5.3]
as the stable Bernstein center.

Under the assumption that
G
is quasi-split, enough is known about the Local Langlands correspondence of the group and existence of tempered
L
-packets, Varma showed in
[
Var24
, Theorem 1.1.5]
that
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)=\mathcal{Z}^{vst}(G)
and hence stable center conjecture is true. Under some additional assumptions (check
[
Var24
, Proposition 1.1.7]
), it was proved in the same article that all three notions of the stable center are the same, i.e.,
\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)=\mathcal{Z}^{vst}(G)=p_{1}^{*}(\mathbb{C}[\Omega(\prescript{L}{}{}G)])
.

Relations to LLC:

Assume that the Local Langlands correspondence is known for
G/k
, and let
\Phi(G/k)
denote the set of Langlands parameters for
G(k)
. If
\phi_{\pi}:WD_{k}\rightarrow\prescript{L}{}{}G
is a Langlands parameter attached to
\pi
by LLC, then the
G^{\vee}
conjugacy class of the restriction
\phi_{\pi}|_{W_{k}}=\lambda_{\pi}:W_{k}\rightarrow\prescript{L}{}{}G)
is called the infinitesimal character attached to
\pi
. We define the infinitesimal class
\tilde{\Pi}(\lambda)
of
\lambda:W_{k}\rightarrow\prescript{L}{}{}G
to be the union of
L
-packets for which the corresponding
L
-parameters restricts to
\lambda
, i.e.,

\tilde{\Pi}(\lambda)=\coprod_{\phi|_{W_{k}=\lambda}}\Pi(\phi)

where
\Pi(\phi)
denotes the
L
-packet corresponding to
\phi\in\Phi(G/k)
. An element
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{st}(G)
conjecturally acts by the same constant on irreducible representations in the same infinitesimal class, i.e,
f_{z}(\pi)=f_{z}(\pi^{\prime})
if
\lambda_{\pi}=\lambda_{\pi^{\prime}}
.

2.4
The fractional depths and subdividing facets

In our earlier work
[
CB24
]
, we gave a description of the integral depth center for simply connected groups. In the present article, we extend the result to general reductive
p
-adic groups, and fractional depths. The description of the integral depth center only needed “data” from the standard parahorics and their integer depth Moy-Prasad filtration subgroups. This is equivalent to fixing an apartment and an alcove(chamber) in it, and using the parahoric subgroups and integer depth filtration subgroups corresponding to the facets (or open polysimplices) in its closure. This is because the parahoric subgroups and their integral depth Moy-Prasad filtration subgroups do not change in the interior of a facet. However, when we start considering fractional depths, the Moy-Prasad filtration subgroups for fractional depth also changes in the interior of a facet, and how it changes depends upon
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
where the depth
r\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Hence, in order to deal with fractional depths, we have to subdivide the facts in the reduced Bruhat-Tits building into smaller parts depending on the same
m
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{S}
be the apartment corresponding to a
k
-split maximal tori
S
, and for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, let
\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})
denote the set of affine functions of the form
\psi+\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}
,
\psi\in\Psi(\mathcal{A})
. These are affine functions of the form
\Phi+\frac{k}{m}
for
\Phi\in\Phi(G,S)
and
\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})\subset\Psi(\mathcal{A})
. We can use the co-dimension one affine subspaces
H_{\psi}=\{x\in\mathcal{A}\>|\>\psi(x)=0\}
for
\psi\in\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})
to get a refined polysimplicial decomposition of
\mathcal{A}
, and hence of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. We denote
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
(resp.
\mathcal{A}
) with the new polysimplicial decomposition
\mathcal{B}_{m}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{m}
), and we call the facets we get in this case refined facets. Let
[\mathcal{B}]
denote the set of facets (or open polysimplices) of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and
[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
denote the set of refined facets obtained by using
\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})
. These are obtained by “subdividing each polysimplex
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}]
into
m^{\mathrm{dim}\>\sigma}
smaller polysimplices”. Similarly, we let
[\mathcal{A}]
(resp.
[\mathcal{A}_{m}]
) denote the set of facets (resp. refined facets) in
\mathcal{A}
for an apartment
\mathcal{A}
, and
[\bar{\mathcal{C}}]
and
[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
the corresponding set for the closure of a chamber
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{A}
.

Let
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, and
x\in\sigma
. For
r\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we can define
G_{\sigma,r}:=G(k)_{\sigma,r}=G(k)_{x,r}
. Since
r\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, the definition does not depend on the choice of
x\in\sigma
. The group
G_{\sigma,r+}=\cup_{s>r}G_{\sigma,s}
is defined in the ususal manner and
G_{\sigma,0}
denotes the parahoric subgroup corresponding to
\sigma
. Further, let
G_{\sigma}=\text{Stab}_{G(k)}(\sigma)
denote the stabilizer of the (refined) facet.

Some notations:

We can define a partial order on
[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
by defining
\sigma\preceq\tau
if
\sigma
is contained in the closure of
\tau
, and we call
\sigma
a face of
\tau
. A facet of dimension
0
is called a vertex, and we denote the set of vertices in
\mathcal{B}_{m}
by
V(\mathcal{B}_{m})
. A subset
\Sigma\subset[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
is a subcomplex if
|\Sigma|=\cup_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\sigma\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is closed. It is convex if
|\Sigma|
is convex.

3
Description of fractional depth Bernstein center

3.1
Stabilization in the fractional depth case

We fix a Haar measure
\mu
of
G(k)
and let
\mathcal{H}(G):=(C_{c}^{\infty}(G),*)
be the Hecke algebra of compactly supported smooth functions on
G(k)
with multiplication given by the convolution product with respect to
\mu
as defined in (
2.3.1
). Let
T
be a
k
-split maximal torus which we fix henceforth, and let
\mathscr{A}:=\mathcal{A}_{T}
be the apartment corresponding to
T
. We also fix a fundamental alcove (chamber)
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
. This is equivalent to fixing a Iwahori subgroup. Let
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
denote it’s closure, and
[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
denote the set of refined facets obtained by subdividing the facets in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
. Note that
[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
is finite. For each
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, we define

\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}^{r}:=C_{c}^{\infty}\left(\frac{G(k)/G_{\sigma,r+}}{G_{\sigma,0}}\right)

to be the algebra (under convolution with respect to
\mu
) of compactly supported smooth functions on
G(k)
which are
G_{\sigma,r+}
bi-invariant and
{G_{\sigma,0}}
conjugation invariant.

We have the partial order on
[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
given by
\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma
if
\sigma^{\prime}
is contained in the closure of
\sigma
, and this gives a partial order on
[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
. For
\sigma^{\prime},\>\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
and
\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma
, we have a map

\displaystyle\phi^{r}_{\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}:\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto f*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}

Further, for any element
n\in\mathcal{N}:=N_{G}(T)(k)
such that
n\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
, if
n\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\preceq\sigma_{2}
, we add morphisms
\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n}:\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{2}}
in the following way

\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n}:\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Ad}(n)}\mathcal{M}^{r}_{n\sigma_{1}}\xrightarrow{\phi^{r}_{n\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{2}}

With the above defined maps, we have an inverse system
\{\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}
and we define
A^{r}(G)
to be the inverse limit of the algebras
\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}
.

A^{r}(G):=\lim_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}

Given
h=\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
, we can define
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
. If
\sigma^{\prime}\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
, then it is already defined. Otherwise,there exists a chamber
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}
such that
\sigma^{\prime}\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
, and
g\in G(k)
such that
g\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
g\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
for some
\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
. In this case, we define
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Ad}_{g}(h_{\sigma})
.

Showing that
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}
is well-defined is slightly subtle. If
g_{1}\mathcal{C}=g_{2}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
g_{1}\sigma_{1}=g_{2}\sigma_{2}=\sigma^{\prime}
for
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
, then
g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
, which means
g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}\in G_{\mathcal{C}}
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{\Omega}=\text{Stab}_{\mathcal{N}}(\Omega)
for any bounded subset
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
. Using results in Section 7.7 in
[
KP23
]
( for example equation
7.7.1,\;7.7.2
and Proposition
7.7.5
) or Proposition
4.6.28(\mathrm{ii})
in
[
BT84
]
, we get that
G_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{C}}G_{\mathcal{C},0}
. Therefore, we can write
g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}=nh
for
\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{C}},\>h\in G_{\mathcal{C},0}
.Our assumptions imply that
n\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
and
n\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
since
G_{\mathcal{C},0}
pointwise stabilizes
\mathcal{C}
and hence
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
. We are trying to show that
\mathrm{Ad}_{g_{2}}(h_{\sigma_{2}})=\mathrm{Ad}_{g_{1}}(h_{\sigma_{1}})\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{Ad}_{g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}}(h_{\sigma_{1}})=h_{\sigma_{2}}
. Since
h_{\sigma_{i}}
is
G_{\sigma_{i},0}
-conjugation invariant and
G_{\sigma_{i},0}\supset G_{\mathcal{C},0}
for
i=1,2
, in order to show that
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}
is well-defined, it is enough to show that
\mathrm{Ad}_{n}(h_{\sigma_{1}})=h_{\sigma_{2}}
for
n\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathcal{N}
such that
n\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
and
n\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
for
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
. Now,
n\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}\preceq\sigma_{2}
, we have a morphism
\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n}:\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{2}}
where
\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n}=\mathrm{Ad}_{n}
in this case since
\phi^{r}_{n\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}=\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2}}=\text{Id}_{\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma_{2}}}
. Since
h=\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
, we have
\phi^{r}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n}(h_{\sigma_{1}})=\mathrm{Ad}_{n}(h_{\sigma_{1}})=h_{\sigma_{2}}
, and we are done. Also, defined this way
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{r}
.

For every finite subset
\Sigma\subset[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, we can associate an element
[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]
to
h\in A^{r}(G)

[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}h_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(G)

(3.1.1)

Note that
\delta_{r}=\{\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
, and
[A^{\Sigma}_{\delta_{r}}]=E^{\Sigma}_{r}
as defined in
[
BKV15
]
. Let
\Theta_{m}
denote the set of non-empty finite convex subcomplexes
\Sigma\subset[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
. Note that
\Theta_{m}
is an inductive system with respect to inclusions.

Theorem 3.1
.

For every
f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
and
h\in A^{r}(G)
, the sequence
\{[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f\}_{\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}}
stabilizes, and hence
\lim_{\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}}\>[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f
is well-defined.

In order to prove this theorem we need generalizations of some results in
[
BKV15
]
. We first recall some notations and definitions.

Some notations:

(i)

Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}
an apartment, and
\sigma\in[\mathcal{A}_{m}]
a chamber (facet of maximal dimension). We use
\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma)
to denote the set of all
\psi\in\Psi(\mathcal{A})
such that
\psi(\sigma)>0
, and
\psi(\sigma^{\prime})=0
for some face
\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma
of co-dimension one. We call
\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma)
the set of simple affine roots relative to
\sigma
.

(ii)

Given
x\in V(\mathcal{B}_{m}),\>s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, we denote by
\Upsilon_{x,s}
the set of all chambers
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
such that for every apartment
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}
containing
\sigma
and
x
and for every
\psi\in\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma)
we have
\psi(x)\leq s
.

(iii)

We denote by
\Gamma_{s}(\sigma^{\prime},x)\subseteq[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
the subcomplex consisting of all
\sigma\in[\mathcal{A}_{m}]
such that for every
\psi\in\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})
satisfying
\psi(\sigma^{\prime})\leq 0
and
\psi(x)\leq s
,we have
\psi(\sigma)\leq 0
. By definition,
\Gamma_{s}(\sigma^{\prime},x)
is convex.

(iv)

From
[
BKV15
, Lemma 4.10]
, there exists a unique minimal face
\sigma^{\prime}=m_{x,s}(\sigma)
of
\sigma
such that
\sigma\in\Gamma_{s}(\sigma^{\prime},x)
. This defines an idempotent map
m_{x,s}:[\mathcal{B}_{m}]\rightarrow[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\sigma,\>\sigma^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
,
x\in V(\mathcal{B}_{m})
and
r,s\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma
and
\sigma\in\Gamma_{s}(\sigma^{\prime},x)
. Further, let
h=\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
. Then we have the equality

h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}

(3.1.2)

Proof.

From Lemma 4.9 in
[
BKV15
]
, we have
\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=\delta_{G_{\sigma^{\prime},r+}}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}
, and hence our assertion for
h=\delta_{r}
. Note that
\sigma,\>\sigma^{\prime}\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
for some chamber
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}
. Then, there exists
g\in G(k)
and
\sigma_{1},\>{\sigma^{\prime}}_{1}\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
,
{\sigma^{\prime}}_{1}\preceq\sigma_{1}
such that
g\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
,
g{\sigma^{\prime}}_{1}=\sigma^{\prime}
and
g\sigma_{1}=\sigma
. Since
h=\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
, we have
h_{\sigma_{1}}=h_{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{1}}*\delta_{G_{\sigma_{1},r+}}
. Further,
h_{\sigma}=\mathrm{Ad}_{g}(h_{\sigma_{1}})
,
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Ad}_{g}(h_{\sigma_{1}^{\prime}})
and
\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}=\mathrm{Ad}_{g}(\delta_{G_{\sigma_{1},r+}})
, which gives us that
h_{\sigma}=h_{{\sigma^{\prime}}}*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}
. Using this fact, we get

\displaystyle h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}

\displaystyle=\left(h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}\right)*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\left(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}\right)

\displaystyle=h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\left(\delta_{G_{\sigma^{\prime},r+}}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}\right)\>\>\text{ (Using Lemma 4.9, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{BKV2}{}{}]})}

\displaystyle=\left(h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\delta_{G_{\sigma^{\prime},r+}}\right)*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}

which finishes the proof.
∎

Our next proposition is a generalization of Proposition 4.14 (a) in
[
BKV15
]
, and the main technical result used in the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
x\in V(\mathcal{B}_{m})
,
r,s\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and let
\Sigma,\>\Sigma^{\prime}\in\Theta_{m}
be such that
x\in\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
and
\Upsilon_{x,s}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
. Let
h=\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
and
[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]
be as defined in (
3.1.1
).

[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=[A^{\Sigma^{\prime}}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}

(3.1.3)

Proof.

Set
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}=\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
. Then our assertion reduces to proving
[A^{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=0
. We can define an equivalence relation on
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
by
\sigma_{1}\sim\sigma_{2}\Leftrightarrow m_{x,s}(\sigma_{1})=m_{x,s}(\sigma_{2})
. Then
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
decomposes as a disjoint union of equivalence classes
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}=\bigsqcup{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}
, where
{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
denotes the equivalence class of
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. Since
m_{x,s}(\tau)\preceq\tau
and
\tau\in\Gamma_{s}(m_{x,s}(\tau),x)
by definition, using Lemma
3.2
, we have

h_{\tau}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=h_{m_{x,s}(\tau)}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}

for every
\tau\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
. Since
m_{x,s}(\tau)=m_{x,s}(\sigma)\>\forall\>\tau\in{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}
, we have

[A^{{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=\left(\sum_{\tau\in{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\tau}\right)\left(h_{m_{x,s}(\sigma)}*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}\right)

From the proof of Proposition 4.14 (a), we see that
\sum_{\tau\in{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\tau}=0
, which proves our assertion.
∎

Using the results stated above, we can complete the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Let
x\in V(\mathcal{B}_{m})\cap[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
. Then, for any
f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
,
\exists s\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
f
is left
G_{x,(r+s)+}
-invariant, i.e.,
f=\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}*f
, and hence we have

[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f=[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}*f

We choose
\Sigma^{\prime}\in\Theta_{m}
such that
x\in\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\Upsilon_{x,s}\subseteq\Sigma^{\prime}
. From proposition
3.3
, we observe that

[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}=[A^{\Sigma^{\prime}}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}

for large enough
\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}
such that
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
, since
\Upsilon_{x,s}
is finite by
[
BKV15
, Lemma 4.4]
. Hence for
\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}
such that
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
, we have

[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f=[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}*f=[A^{\Sigma^{\prime}}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,(r+s)+}}*f=[A^{\Sigma^{\prime}}_{h}]*f

which proves that
\{[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f\}_{\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}}
stabilizes.
∎

3.2
A limit description of the fractional depth center

Since
\lim_{\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}}\>[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f
is well-defined, we can define

[A_{h}]\in\text{End}_{\mathcal{H}(G)^{op}}(\mathcal{H}(G))
by the formula

[A_{h}](f):=\lim_{\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}}\>[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*f.

(3.2.1)

Remark 3.4
.

For
h=\delta_{r}\in A^{r}(G)
, we have from
[
BKV15
]
that
[A_{\delta_{r}}]
is the projector to the depth-
r
part of the Bernstein center.

Proposition 3.5
.

For every
r\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}
,
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
h\in A^{r}(G)
, we have

[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}=h_{\sigma}.

(3.2.2)

Proof.

Choose
x\in V(\mathcal{B}_{m})
such that
x\preceq\sigma
. Then
G_{x,r+}\subseteq G_{\sigma,r+}
, and we have
\delta_{G_{x,r+}}*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}=\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}
and
h_{x}*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}=h_{\sigma}
. Hence, it is enough to show that
[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,r+}}=h_{x}
. Since
\Upsilon_{x,0}=\emptyset
by
[
BKV15
, Lemma 4.4]
, the subcomplex
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{x\}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
3.3
with
s=0
. Hence, we have

[A^{\Sigma}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,r+}}=[A^{\{x\}}_{h}]*\delta_{G_{x,r+}}=h_{x}*\delta_{G_{x,r+}}=h_{x}

and we are done.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Note that the above proposition implies that for
h\in A^{r}(G)
and
\forall\>\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, we have
[A_{h}](\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})=h_{\sigma}
.

Theorem 3.7
.

For each
h\in A^{r}(G)
, we have
[A_{h}]\in\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)\subset\mathcal{Z}(G)\simeq\emph{End}_{\mathcal{H}(G)^{2}}(\mathcal{H}(G))
, and the assignment
h\mapsto[A_{h}]
defines an algebra map

\displaystyle[A^{r}]\>:\>A^{r}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\longmapsto[A_{h}]

Proof.

We first show that
[A_{h}]\in\text{End}_{\mathcal{H}(G)^{2}}(\mathcal{H}(G))
, and then prove that the map defined in an algebra homomorphism. For the first part, it can be easily observed from the definition of
[A_{h}]
that
[A_{h}](f*g)=[A_{h}](f)*g
for
h\in A^{r}(G)
. Since,
[A_{h}]
commutes with right convolutions, it is enough to show that it is
G(k)
-conjugation equivariant.

Let
L
be a compact open subgroup of
G(k)
. Given any
l\in L
and
f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
, we can choose
\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}
large enough such that
[A_{h}](f)=A^{\Sigma}_{h}*f
and
[A_{h}](\mathrm{Ad}_{l}(f))=A^{\Sigma}_{h}*\mathrm{Ad}_{l}(f)
, since
A^{\Sigma}_{h}*f
stabilizes
\forall\>f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
. Then, we can choose the
\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma^{L}\in\Theta_{m}
such that
\Sigma^{L}
is Ad
L
-invariant. In that case,
A^{\Sigma^{L}}_{h}
is Ad
L
-invariant and we have

\mathrm{Ad}_{l}([A_{h}](f))=\mathrm{Ad}_{l}(A^{\Sigma^{L}}_{h}*f)=\mathrm{Ad}_{l}(A^{\Sigma^{L}}_{h})*\mathrm{Ad}_{l}(f)=A^{\Sigma^{L}}_{h}*\mathrm{Ad}_{l}(f)=[A_{h}](\mathrm{Ad}_{l}(f)).

So, we have shown that
[A_{h}]
is Ad
L
-invariant for any compact open subgroup
L\subseteq G(k)
.
By functoriality of buildings (
[
Lan00
, Theorem 2.1.8]
), the natural projection
p:G\rightarrow G^{\text{ad}}
induces a bijection
\mathcal{B}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(G^{\text{ad}})
which is compatible with
G(k)
-action on the left hand side and
G^{\text{ad}}(k)
-action on the right hand side. In particular, this means
G(k)
acts on
\mathcal{B}(G)
via
G^{\text{ad}}(k)
, i.e.,
g\cdot x=p_{k}(g)\cdot x
for
g\in G(k),\>x\in\mathcal{B}(G)=\mathcal{B}(G^{\text{ad}})
. So, it is enough to prove that
[A_{h}]
is
G^{\text{ad}}(k)
-conjugation invariant. Since
G^{\text{ad}}(k)
is generated by compact open subgroups (
[
BKV15
]
, 6.1), we have that
[A_{h}]
is
G(k)
-conjugation invariant and hence
[A_{h}]\in\text{End}_{\mathcal{H}(G)^{2}}(\mathcal{H}(G))
.

Given
h,h^{\prime}\in A^{r}(G)
and
f\in\mathcal{H}(G)
, we will show that
[A_{h*h^{\prime}}](f)=([A_{h}]\circ[A_{h^{\prime}}])(f)
. Choose
\Sigma\in\Theta_{m}
large enough such that
[A_{h*h^{\prime}}](f)=A^{\Sigma}_{h*h^{\prime}}*f
and
[A_{h^{\prime}}](f)=A^{\Sigma}_{h^{\prime}}*f
. Then,
([A_{h}]\circ[A_{h^{\prime}}])(f)=[A_{h}]([A_{h^{\prime}}](f))=[A_{h}](A^{\Sigma}_{h^{\prime}}*f)=[A_{h}](A^{\Sigma}_{h^{\prime}})*f
, and it is enough to show that
A^{\Sigma}_{h*h^{\prime}}=[A_{h}](A^{\Sigma}_{h^{\prime}})
. Now, using the fact that
[A_{h}]
is linear and the definitions
A^{\Sigma}_{h*h^{\prime}}=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}h_{\sigma}*{h^{\prime}}_{\sigma}
,
A^{\Sigma}_{h^{\prime}}=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}{h^{\prime}}_{\sigma}
, we have

\displaystyle[A_{h}](A^{\Sigma}_{h^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}[A_{h}]({h^{\prime}}_{\sigma})=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}[A_{h}](\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}*{h^{\prime}}_{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}[A_{h}](\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})*{h^{\prime}}_{\sigma}=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}(-1)^{\text{dim}\>\sigma}h_{\sigma}*{h^{\prime}}_{\sigma}

which shows that
[A_{h}]
is an algebra map. Further, for
h\in A^{r}(G)
,

[A_{h}]=[A^{r}](h)=[A^{r}](h*\delta_{r})=[A_{h*\delta_{r}}]=[A_{h}]\circ[A_{\delta_{r}}]

and hence
[A_{h}]\in\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
since
[A_{\delta_{r}}]
is the depth-
r
projector.

∎

Theorem 3.8
.

The map
[A^{r}]:A^{r}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
defined in Theorem
3.7
is an algebra isomorphism onto the depth-
r
Bernstein centre.

Proof.

We prove the assertion in two steps. We first construct a section of this map and then show that it is actually an inverse algebra map.

Claim 3.8.1
.

We have an algebra map
\Psi^{r}\>:\>\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)\longrightarrow A^{r}(G)
such that
\Psi^{r}\circ[A^{r}]=Id_{A^{r}(G)}

For
\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
, we define

\displaystyle\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}\>:\>\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto z_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})

We easily see that
\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}
is well-defined since
G_{\sigma,r+}
is normal in
G_{\sigma,0}
. Let
\pi^{r}_{\sigma}:A^{r}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{r}_{\sigma}
be the canonical projection map. For
\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
we have
\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}=\phi^{r}_{\sigma^{\prime},\sigma}\circ\Psi^{r}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
. So, there exists a map
\lim_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}=:\Psi_{r}:\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)\longrightarrow A^{r}(G)
such that
\pi^{r}_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{r}=\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
. Also note that each
\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}
is an algebra map. Given
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
,

\displaystyle(z\circ z^{\prime})_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})

\displaystyle=z_{\mathcal{H}}\circ{z^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})=z_{\mathcal{H}}({z^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}))

\displaystyle=z_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}*{z^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}))=z_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})*{z^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})

Hence,
\Psi^{r}:\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)\rightarrow A^{r}(G)
is an algebra map. Finally, for
h=\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
, we have using Remark
3.6
that
\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}([A_{h}])=[A_{h}](\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})=h_{\sigma}

\forall\>\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
. Hence,
\Psi^{r}\circ[A^{r}](h)=\Psi^{r}([A_{h}])=h
, which finishes the proof of this claim.

Note that the above claim implies that
\Psi^{r}
is surjective and
[A^{r}]
is injective as algebra maps. So, if we can show injectivity of
\Psi^{r}
, we can conclude that
\Psi^{r}
and
[A^{r}]
are inverse algebra isomorphisms.

Claim 3.8.2
.

\Psi^{r}
is injective.

Assume
\Psi^{r}(z)=\Psi^{r}(z^{\prime})
for
z,\>z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
and let
(\pi,\;V)\in\mathrm{Irr}(G)_{\leq r}
.
\exists\>\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
such that
V^{G_{\sigma,r+}}\ni v\neq\{0\}
. Then
\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}(v)=v
. In order to show
z=z^{\prime}
, it is enough to show
z_{V}(v)={z^{\prime}}_{V}(v)
. Note that
z_{V}(v)=z_{V}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}(v))=z_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})(v)
and the same is true for
z^{\prime}
. Since
\Psi^{r}(z)=\Psi^{r}(z^{\prime})
, we have

\pi^{r}_{\sigma}\circ\Psi^{r}(z)=\pi^{r}_{\sigma}\circ\Psi^{r}(z^{\prime})\Rightarrow\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}(z)=\Psi^{r}_{\sigma}(z^{\prime})\Rightarrow z_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})={z^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})

\forall\;\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
. Hence,

z_{V}(v)=z_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})(v)={z^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{H}}(\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}})(v)={z^{\prime}}_{V}(v)

which proves injectivity of
\Psi^{r}
and gives us an isomorphism.
∎

We have isomorphisms
[A^{r}]:A^{r}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
for all
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
. For any
r,\;s\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0},\>r>s,\>r\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z},\;s\in\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}
, we have a map

\displaystyle e_{r,s}\>:\>A^{r}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow A^{s}(G)

\displaystyle\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{h^{\prime}_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}]}

where
h^{\prime}_{\tau}=h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}
for any
\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]
such that
\tau\cap\sigma\neq\emptyset
. Let
A_{\tau}=\{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]\>|\>\sigma\cap\tau\neq\emptyset\}
and
A^{max}_{\tau}=\{\sigma\in A_{\tau}\>|\>\sigma\text{ of maximal dimension }\}
. We claim that
h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}=h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}
for all
\sigma,\>\sigma^{\prime}\in A_{\tau}
and hence the map
e_{r,s}
is well-defined. If
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in A^{max}_{\tau}
with
x\in\sigma\cap\tau,\>x^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\prime}\cap\tau
, then without loss of generality we can assume that there exists
\tilde{\sigma}
such that
\tilde{\sigma}\preceq\sigma
and
\tilde{\sigma}\preceq\sigma^{\prime}
, and hence
\tilde{\sigma}\in A_{\tau}
. In that case,

h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}=h_{\tilde{\sigma}}*\delta_{G_{\sigma,r+}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}=h_{\tilde{\sigma}}*\delta_{G_{x,r+}}*\delta_{G_{x,s+}}=h_{\tilde{\sigma}}*\delta_{G_{x,s+}}=h_{\tilde{\sigma}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}

and similarly
h_{\sigma^{\prime}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}=h_{\tilde{\sigma}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}=h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}
. Now, if
\sigma\in A_{\tau}
, there exists
\tilde{\sigma}\in A^{max}_{\tau}
with
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{\sigma}\cap\tau
such that
\sigma\preceq\tilde{\sigma}
which gives us

h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}=h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tilde{x},s+}}=h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tilde{x},r+}}*\delta_{G_{\tilde{x},s+}}=h_{\sigma}*\delta_{G_{\tilde{\sigma},r+}}*\delta_{G_{\tilde{x},s+}}=h_{\tilde{\sigma}}*\delta_{G_{\tilde{x},s+}}=h_{\tilde{\sigma}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}

and hence we are done. We define

A(G):=\lim_{r\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}}A^{r}(G)=\lim_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}A^{r}(G)

(3.2.3)

where the second limit is taken with respect to the maps
e_{r+1,r}
.

Further, note that we have a natural map

\displaystyle z_{r,s}\>:\>\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{s}(G)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto z\circ[A_{\delta_{s}}]

such that
\mathcal{Z}(G)=\lim_{r\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}}\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)=\lim_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
r,s
be as in the preceding paragraphs. The algebra isomorphisms
[A^{r}]:A^{r}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)
fit into the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{A^{r}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[A^{r}]}
\scriptstyle{e_{r,s}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{r}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z_{r,s}}
\textstyle{A^{s}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[A^{s}]}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{s}(G)}

In particular, we have an algebra isomorphism

[A]=\lim_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}[A^{r}]:A(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}(G).

(3.2.4)

Proof.

Let
\{h_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{m}]}\in A^{r}(G)
and
h^{\prime}=e_{r,s}(h)=\{h_{\sigma_{\tau}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}\}_{\tau\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}]}
where
\sigma_{\tau}\in A_{\tau}
. We want to show that
[A_{h^{\prime}}]=[A_{h}]\circ[A_{\delta_{s}}]
. Let
(\pi,V)
be a smooth irreducible representation of
G(k)
. If the depth of
\pi
is
\leq s
, then
\exists\>0\neq v\in V^{G_{\tau,s+}}
,
\tau\in[\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{n}]
. In this case,

[A_{h^{\prime}}](v)=[A_{h^{\prime}}](\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}}(v))=[A_{h^{\prime}}](\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}})(v)=(h_{\sigma_{\tau}}*\delta_{G_{\tau,s+}})v=h_{\sigma_{\tau}}(v).

Further, fix
\sigma_{\tau}\in A_{\tau}
and let
x\in\sigma_{\tau}\cap\tau
. Then
v\in V^{G_{x,s+}}\supset V^{G_{x,r+}}
since
r>s
, and

[A_{h}]\circ[A_{\delta_{s}}](v)=[A_{h}](v)=[A_{h}](\delta_{G_{x,r+}}(v))=[A_{h}](\delta_{G_{x,r+}})v=[A_{h}](\delta_{G_{\sigma_{\tau},r+}})v=h_{\sigma_{\tau}}(v).

So, we have
[A_{h^{\prime}}]|_{V}=([A_{h}]\circ[A_{\delta_{r}}])|_{V}
when the depth of
\pi
is less than
s
.

When depth of
\pi
is
>s
,
[A_{h^{\prime}}]|_{V}=0=([A_{h}]\circ[A_{\delta_{s}}])|_{V}
. The rest follows immediately.
∎

4
Stable functions on positive depth Moy-Prasad quotients

For
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
, let
\mathbb{G}_{\sigma}
be the connected reductive
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
- group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with associated Frobenius
F
such that
\mathbb{G}_{\sigma}(\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q})=G(K)_{\sigma,0}/G(K)_{\sigma,0+}
. We know that
\mathbb{G}_{\sigma}
is the reductive quotient of the special fibre of the parahoric group scheme corresponding to
\sigma
. Further, let
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}_{\sigma}=\text{Lie}(\mathbb{G}_{\sigma})
and
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}_{\sigma,r}
be the
\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
vector space such that
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}_{\sigma,r}(\bar{\mathbb{F}}_{q})=G(K)_{\sigma,r}/G(K)_{\sigma,r+}
for
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
. It has a
\mathbb{F}_{q}
- structure induced by
G_{\sigma,r}/G_{\sigma,r+}\cong\bar{\mathfrak{g}}_{\sigma,r}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Note that
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}_{\sigma,r}\cong\bar{\mathfrak{g}}_{\sigma}
for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. The action of
G_{\sigma,0}/G_{\sigma,0+}
on
G_{\sigma,r}/G_{\sigma,r+}
for
r\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
is the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-points of a linear algebraic action of
\mathbb{G}_{\sigma}
on
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}_{\sigma,r}
. For the special case where
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, this is isomorphic to the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-points of the adjoint action of
\mathbb{G}_{\sigma}
on its Lie algebra, and we studied stable functions on these Moy-Prasad quotients at positive integral depth in
[
CB24
]
. The goal of this section is to study stable functions more generally allowing fractional depth quotients as well.

4.1
Fractional depth quotients

Fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and let
0<r\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. We will define an analogue of Iwahori decomposition following
[
MS20
]
. Let
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
and
\mathcal{A}
be an apartment containing
\sigma
. We define a set
\Phi_{\sigma}

\Phi^{m}_{\sigma}=\Phi_{\sigma}:=\{\nabla\psi\>|\>\psi\in\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A}),\>\psi=r\text{ on }\sigma\}

(4.1.1)

This is a root-subsystem of
\Phi
, and is independent of
r
(depends only on
m
). For
\sigma,\;\tau\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
such that
\sigma\preceq\tau
,
\Phi_{\tau}\subset\Phi_{\sigma}
is a root subsystem.
Some specific examples of
\Phi_{\sigma}
:

•

If the facet
\sigma
is a hyperspecial point,
\Phi_{\sigma}=\Phi
.

•

If
\sigma
is a chamber,
\Phi_{\sigma}=\emptyset

Observe that if
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\;m|n
and for
\sigma^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{B}_{n}]
, there exists unique
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
such that
\sigma^{\prime}\subset\sigma
and
\Phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{n}\supset\Phi_{\sigma}^{m}=:\Phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{m}
and
\Phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{m}
defined this way is well-defined and a root subsystem of
\Phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{n}
. In particular,
\Phi^{1}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\subset\Phi^{n}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Further, for
x\in\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}
, we can define
\Phi^{n}_{x}
the following way-

\Phi_{x}^{n}=\{\nabla\psi\>|\>\psi\in\Psi_{n}(\mathcal{A}),\>\psi(x)=\frac{1}{n}\}

(4.1.2)

Defined this way, we have
\Phi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{n}=\cap_{x\in\sigma^{\prime}}\Phi_{x}^{n}
for
\sigma^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{A}_{n}]\subset[\mathcal{B}_{n}]
.

For
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
contained in some apartment
\mathcal{A}
, let
\text{Aff}(\sigma)
denote the affine space generated by
\sigma
in
\mathcal{A}
. If
X
is a real affine space and
Y
is an affine subspace of
X
, there is a unique affine orthogonal transformation
R_{Y}
which reflects points of
X
across
Y
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\sigma\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
. Two refined facets
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in[\mathcal{B}_{m}]
such that
\sigma\preceq\tau,\>\sigma\preceq\tau^{\prime}
are said to be opposite with respect to
\sigma
(or
\sigma
-opposite) if there exists an apartment
\mathcal{A}
containing both
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
such that in
\mathcal{A}
:

(i)

The affine subspaces Aff
(\tau)
and Aff
(\tau^{\prime})
are equal.

(ii)

The reflection
R_{\text{Aff}(\sigma)}(\tau)=\tau^{\prime}

Let
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
be
\sigma
-opposite and
\tau,\;\tau^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}
. We immediately observe that if
\psi\in\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\psi=r
on
\tau
, then
\psi=r
on
\tau^{\prime}
and vice-versa. Hence,
\Phi_{\tau}=\Phi_{\tau^{\prime}}
. Further,
G_{\tau,r}/G_{\tau,r+}\cong G_{\tau^{\prime},r}/G_{\tau^{\prime},r+}
. For
\psi\in\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\psi=r
on
\sigma
, there are two possibilities for
\nabla\psi
:

(i)

\psi=r
on
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
, and hence
\nabla\psi\in\Phi_{\tau}=\Phi_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

(ii)

\psi
is non-constant on
\tau
(and
\tau^{\prime}
), which presents us with two possibilities.
\psi>r
on
\tau
and
\psi<r
on
\tau^{\prime}
and we denote these by
\Phi_{\sigma,\tau}
, and
\psi>r
on
\tau^{\prime}
and
\psi<r
on
\tau
, which we denote by
\Phi_{\sigma,\tau^{\prime}}
. This basically gives us a choice of positive roots in the root system
\Phi_{\sigma}
, and
\Phi_{\sigma,\tau}=-\Phi_{\sigma,\tau^{\prime}}
. Further,
\Phi_{\sigma}
is a disjoint union

\Phi_{\sigma}=\Phi_{\tau}\;\sqcup\Phi_{\sigma,\tau}\;\sqcup\Phi_{\sigma,\tau^{\prime}}

(4.1.3)

For
\psi\in\Psi_{m}(\mathcal{A})
, the quotient
\mathscr{L}_{\psi}:=U_{\psi}(K)/U_{\psi+}(K)
